```json
{
  "intent": "Determine whether the hand slowness is a psychogenic symptom (consistent with Major Depressive Disorder with somatic features) or a neurological symptom that warrants further investigation. My brain knows how to do the normal motions, but my hands just won't move at their normal speed, so everything just takes a lot more effort and time to get through.\n\nDoctor: Besides the slowness in your hands, have you noticed any difficulty with your balance, or do you find yourself moving more slowly overall—like when you're walking or getting up from a chair?\n\nPatient: Um, my balance is fine, I haven't noticed any problems with that. And as for walking or getting up... I don't really think I'm moving slower overall. \n\nLike I mentioned, it really just feels like it's my hands at work. That's what people at the salon are noticing—just that I'm a lot slower at doing those specific things than I used to be.\n\nDoctor: When you try to move your hands, do they feel stiff or rigid, or does it feel more like you're just moving through resistance without the stiffness?\n\nPatient: Um, no, they don't really feel stiff or rigid. It's not like my joints are locked up or anything. It's just... like I said, it really does just feel like they're moving through glue. There's just this heavy resistance when I'm at work, and doing things like sectioning the hair or mixing the color just takes me forever now. It's just that slowness that everyone's noticing, not really a stiffness.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few weeks or months, have you been feeling sad, down, or depressed, or noticed changes in your mood or energy levels in general?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. It's really just at work. That's where it's the most obvious. Like I was saying, my hands just feel like they're moving through glue when I'm there. It's just kind of always there when I'm working. Like I said, it just feels like my hands are moving through glue. When I'm texting or eating, or doing stuff like that, I don't really notice it. It's just when I'm doing hair. Like I was saying, it's the sectioning and the mixing color where my hands just feel like they're moving through glue. That's what takes me forever, and that's what the other people at the salon are noticing. It's just... I'm just slower at my job now.\n\nDoctor: Before you came to see me today, did you go to your regular doctor or any other doctors about this heavy feeling in your hands and the slowness? My brain knows how to do the normal motions, but my hands just won't move at their normal speed, so everything just takes a lot more effort and time to get through.\n\nDoctor: Besides the slowness in your hands, have you noticed any difficulty with your balance, or do you find yourself moving more slowly overall—like when you're walking or getting up from a chair?\n\nPatient: Um, my balance is fine, I haven't noticed any problems with that. And as for walking or getting up... I don't really think I'm moving slower overall. \n\nLike I mentioned, it really just feels like it's my hands at work. That's where it's the most obvious, when I'm trying to section hair or mix the color and it just takes me forever. That's what people at the salon are noticing—just that I'm a lot slower at doing those specific things than I used to be.\n\nDoctor: When you try to move your hands, do they feel stiff or rigid, or does it feel more like you're just moving through resistance without the stiffness?\n\nPatient: Um, no, they don't really feel stiff or rigid. It's not like my joints are locked up or anything. It's just... like I said, it really does just feel like they're moving through glue. There's just this heavy resistance when I'm at work, and doing things like sectioning the hair or mixing the color just takes me forever now. It's just that slowness that everyone's noticing, not really a stiffness.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few weeks or months, have you been feeling sad, down, or depressed, or noticed changes in your mood or energy levels in general?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. For the past few months, honestly. Most days, I just wake up with this... heavy, flat sadness right in my chest. It just sits there. It feels like... I don't know, like there's just no point in trying to build the life I was planning on having. It's just hard to care about much of anything right now.\n\nDoctor: When you're not at work—like at home, doing things around the house or in your free time—do you notice that same heavy, sluggish feeling in your hands, or is it really just something that happens when you're working at the salon?\n\nPatient: Um... honestly, I don't really notice it much at home. It's really just at work. That's where it's the most obvious. Like I was saying, my hands just feel like they're moving through glue when I'm there. Even just the basic stuff, like sectioning hair or mixing color... it just takes me forever now. It's gotten to the point where people at the salon have actually started noticing that I'm just a lot slower than I used to be. But at home, no, it's not really like that.\n\nDoctor: When you're at work and you notice your hands feeling slow, does the slowness get worse when you're stressed or upset about something, or does it stay about the same regardless of how you're feeling emotionally at that moment?\n\nPatient: Um, I don't think it really changes based on how I'm feeling. It's just kind of always there when I'm working. Like I said, it just feels like my hands are moving through glue. Whether I'm stressed or just having a normal day, sectioning the hair or mixing the color just takes me forever now. It stays pretty much the same, and that's why people at the salon have started noticing that I'm just slower than I used to be.\n\nDoctor: When you're at work and your hands feel slow, if you suddenly get really focused on something—like if a favorite customer came in and you were excited to do their hair—does that heavy, sluggish feeling in your hands go away or get better, or does it stay just as strong no matter what?\n\nPatient: No, it doesn't really go away. Even if it's someone I really like, or a cut I usually enjoy doing... it doesn't matter. My hands still just feel like they're moving through glue. It's like my body just won't cooperate, no matter how I'm feeling about the appointment. I'm still just so slow at sectioning the hair and mixing the color, and it still takes me forever. Like I said, it's gotten bad enough that my coworkers have pointed out how much slower I am now. It just doesn't change.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing other things that require fine hand movements—like writing, eating with a fork, buttoning buttons, or texting on your phone—do your hands feel that same heavy, glue-like slowness, or is it really just limited to the hair styling tasks at work?\n\nPatient: Um... no, it's really just at work. When I'm texting or eating, or doing stuff like that, I don't really notice it. It's just when I'm doing hair. Like I was saying, it's the sectioning and the mixing color where my hands just feel like they're moving through glue. That's what takes me forever, and that's what the other people at the salon are noticing. It's just... Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
```
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I haven't seen them physically do it, like standing at the computer or whatever. But I just know it's happening. I mean, it's too much of a coincidence, right? Every single time I mention out loud that I need to save up for something or that money is tight, my schedule suddenly clears out for the next few days. It's like they're listening and then deliberately going in and messing with my hours to make sure I don't make any money. I know it sounds strange when I say it out loud, but I'm just pretty sure someone is doing it on purpose.
